I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a bracket sequence with only '(' and ')'
2. We want to make it a correct (regular) bracket sequence
3. We can perform "reorder" operations on any consecutive substring, which takes `l` nanoseconds where `l` is the length of the substring
4. We need to find the minimum total nanoseconds to make the sequence correct, or return -1 if impossible

## Key Insights

1. **Impossibility condition**: A bracket sequence can be made correct only if the total number of opening brackets equals the total number of closing brackets. If they're not equal, return -1.

2. **What does a reorder operation do?**: It allows us to rearrange any substring arbitrarily. This means we can transform any substring into any permutation of its characters.

3. **Optimal strategy**: The key insight is that we only need to fix the positions where brackets are mismatched. In a correct bracket sequence, at any point when reading left to right, the number of opening brackets should be greater than or equal to the number of closing brackets.

4. **Greedy approach**: We can think of this as fixing "deficiencies" in the bracket sequence. When we have too many closing brackets early, we need to move some opening brackets forward.

5. **Optimal solution**: We can use a greedy approach where we identify the "bad" segments and compute the cost efficiently.

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. First check if it's possible - count opening and closing brackets. If they're not equal, return -1.

2. For a sequence to be correct, at no point should we have more closing brackets than opening brackets when reading left to right.

3. We can use the concept of "balance" - we track the running balance as we go from left to right.

4. When the balance goes negative, it means we have more closing brackets than opening brackets at that point, which is invalid.

5. For each section where the balance goes negative, we need to fix it by moving opening brackets forward.

6. The key insight is that we can group consecutive segments that need fixing and calculate the minimum cost.

## Final Algorithm

1. Count total opening and closing brackets. If not equal, return -1.
2. Compute running balance (starting from 0)
3. For each position, if balance is negative, add its absolute value to the answer
4. Return the total answer

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count opening and closing brackets
open_count = s.count('(')
close_count = s.count(')')

# If they are not equal, it's impossible
if open_count != close_count:
    print(-1)
else:
    balance = 0
    total_cost = 0
    
    # Calculate running balance and accumulate cost of negative balances
    for char in s:
        if char == '(':
            balance += 1
        else:  # char == ')'
            balance -= 1
        
        # If balance is negative, add its absolute value to cost
        if balance < 0:
            total_cost += abs(balance)
    
    print(total_cost)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a bracket sequence with only '(' and ')'
2. We want to make it a correct (regular) bracket sequence
3. We can perform "reorder" operations on any consecutive substring
4. Each reorder operation on a substring of length `l` takes `l` nanoseconds
5. We need to find the minimum total time to make the sequence correct, or return -1 if impossible

## Key Insights

1. **Impossibility condition**: A bracket sequence can be made correct only if the total number of opening brackets equals the total number of closing brackets. If they're not equal, return -1.

2. **What does a reorder operation do?**: It allows us to rearrange any consecutive substring in any order. This means we can transform any substring into any permutation of its characters.

3. **Strategy**: We need to identify which parts of the sequence need to be "fixed" to make it balanced.

4. **Key observation**: In a correct bracket sequence, at any prefix position, the number of opening brackets should be at least the number of closing brackets, and the total count should be equal.

5. **Optimal approach**: We need to find the minimum cost to balance the sequence. The cost is the sum of lengths of substrings we reorder.

## Algorithm Development

1. First, check if the total number of '(' equals the number of ')'. If not, return -1.

2. We need to make the sequence balanced. The key insight is that we can think of this as finding "deficit" and "surplus" positions.

3. For a sequence to be balanced, at each position i (0-indexed), if we have a prefix ending at i, the number of '(' should be ≥ number of ')'.

4. We can use a greedy approach: identify positions where we have too many closing brackets or too few opening brackets.

5. Actually, a more efficient insight: we can think of this as we need to "transfer" opening brackets to positions where we have excess closing brackets.

6. The minimum cost can be found by considering the "balance" at each position and identifying segments that need to be reordered.

## Step-by-step Solution

After analyzing the problem, here's the key insight:

- We need to ensure that at no point does the number of closing brackets exceed the number of opening brackets in any prefix.
- We can use the concept of "net balance" - at each position, balance = count('(') - count(')')
- For the sequence to be valid, balance should never go negative and should end at 0.

The algorithm:

1. Check if total '(' == total ')'. If not, return -1.
2. Calculate the balance at each position.
3. Find all positions where the balance goes negative - these are problematic.
4. For each segment where we have negative balance, we need to "fix" it by moving opening brackets to those positions.
5. The optimal way is to find the minimum cost to balance all the segments.

Actually, let me think of this differently:

The key insight is that we need to make sure the running balance never goes negative. This means we need to have enough opening brackets at the right positions.

We can use a greedy approach:
- Find all positions where the running balance becomes negative
- For each such position, we need to "add" opening brackets to make the balance non-negative
- The cost is the sum of lengths of segments we need to reorder

4. The minimum cost is related to how many positions have negative balance and the distances between them.

Actually, I recall that this problem is similar to the "minimum cost to make bracket sequence balanced" where we can reorder segments.

The optimal solution:
- We need to ensure the running balance never goes negative
- For each position i, if the running balance becomes negative, we need to "fix" this by reordering a segment that includes this position

The insight is that we can think of it as having "deficits" at certain positions, and we can transfer opening brackets from segments that have surplus.

1. Check if total opening == total closing
2. Calculate the minimum cost using the negative balance positions

```python
n = int(input())

# Count total opening and closing brackets

close_count = s.count(')')

    print(-1)

    balance = 0
    negative_positions = []

            balance += 1
        else:
            balance -= 1

            cost = 0
```

```python

    cost = 0

            balance += 1
        else:
            balance -= 1
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

A bracketed sequence is called correct (regular) if by inserting "+" and "1" you can get a well-formed mathematical expression from it. For example, sequences "(())()", "()" and "(()(()))" are correct, while ")(", "(()" and "(()))(" are not.

The teacher gave Dmitry's class a very strange task — she asked every student to come up with a sequence of arbitrary length, consisting only of opening and closing brackets. After that all the students took turns naming the sequences they had invented. When Dima's turn came, he suddenly realized that all his classmates got the correct bracketed sequence, and whether he got the correct bracketed sequence, he did not know.

Dima suspects now that he simply missed the word "correct" in the task statement, so now he wants to save the situation by modifying his sequence slightly. More precisely, he can the arbitrary number of times (possibly zero) perform the reorder operation.

The reorder operation consists of choosing an arbitrary consecutive subsegment (substring) of the sequence and then reordering all the characters in it in an arbitrary way. Such operation takes l nanoseconds, where l is the length of the subsegment being reordered. It's easy to see that reorder operation doesn't change the number of opening and closing brackets. For example for "))((" he can choose the substring ")(" and do reorder ")()(" (this operation will take 2 nanoseconds).

Since Dima will soon have to answer, he wants to make his sequence correct as fast as possible. Help him to do this, or determine that it's impossible.

Input

The first line contains a single integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^6) — the length of Dima's sequence.

The second line contains string of length n, consisting of characters "(" and ")" only.

Output

Print a single integer — the minimum number of nanoseconds to make the sequence correct or "-1" if it is impossible to do so.

Examples

Input


8
))((())(


Output


6


Input


3
(()


Output


-1

Note

In the first example we can firstly reorder the segment from first to the fourth character, replacing it with "()()", the whole sequence will be "()()())(". And then reorder the segment from the seventh to eighth character, replacing it with "()". In the end the sequence will be "()()()()", while the total time spent is 4 + 2 = 6 nanoseconds.

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a bracket sequence with only '(' and ')'
2. We want to make it a correct (regular) bracket sequence
3. We can perform "reorder" operations on any consecutive substring - we can rearrange its characters in any order
4. Each operation on a substring of length `l` takes `l` nanoseconds
5. We need to find the minimum total time to make the sequence correct, or return -1 if impossible

## Key Insights

1. **When is a bracket sequence impossible to fix?**
   - For a bracket sequence to be possible, the total number of opening brackets must equal the total number of closing brackets
   - If opening brackets ≠ closing brackets, return -1

2. **What does a reorder operation allow us to do?**
   - We can arbitrarily rearrange any consecutive substring
   - This means we can transform any substring into any permutation of its characters

3. **Optimal strategy:**
   - We need to fix the bracket balance
   - The key insight is that we want to minimize the total length of operations
   - We should focus on regions where the bracket balance goes negative or exceeds what's needed

4. **Greedy approach:**
   - First, calculate the prefix sum of brackets (where '(' adds +1, ')' adds -1)
   - A valid sequence must have all prefix sums ≥ 0 and total sum = 0
   - Any negative prefix sum means we have too many closing brackets at that point
   - For each negative prefix sum, we need to fix it by moving opening brackets forward

5. **Optimal operation strategy:**
   - When we have a negative prefix sum at position i, it means we have more closing than opening brackets in the prefix up to i
   - We need to "move" some opening brackets to this region
   - The optimal way is to find the smallest contiguous segment containing both the negative region and some opening brackets to fix it

- Check if opening == closing

```python
n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count opening and closing brackets
opening = s.count('(')
closing = s.count(')')

# If total opening brackets != total closing brackets, impossible
if opening != closing:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Calculate prefix sums
    prefix_sum = 0
    total_cost = 0
    
    for char in s:
        if char == '(':
            prefix_sum += 1
        else:  # char == ')'
            prefix_sum -= 1
        
        # If prefix sum is negative, add its absolute value to cost
        if prefix_sum < 0:
            total_cost += abs(prefix_sum)
    
    print(total_cost)
```

n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

# Count opening and closing brackets
opening = s.count('(')
closing = s.count(')')

# If total opening brackets != total closing brackets, impossible
if opening != closing:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Calculate prefix sums
    prefix_sum = 0
    total_cost = 0
    
    for char in s:
        if char == '(':
            prefix_sum += 1
        else:  # char == ')'
            prefix_sum -= 1
        
        # If prefix sum is negative, add its absolute value to cost
        if prefix_sum < 0:
            total_cost += abs(prefix_sum)
    
    print(total_cost)

* Operation: Choose one of the strings A, B and C, and specify an integer i between 1 and N (inclusive). Change the i-th character from the beginning of the chosen string to some other lowercase English letter.



What is the minimum number of operations required to achieve the objective?

Constraints

* Each of the strings A, B and C is a string of length N.
* Each character in each of the strings A, B and C is a lowercase English letter.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have three strings A, B, and C of length N. We want to make all three strings identical by changing characters. In each operation, we can change one character in one of the three strings to any other lowercase letter.

The key insight is that for each position i (from 0 to N-1), we need to determine how many changes are needed to make all three strings have the same character at that position.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

For each position i:
- If all three strings have the same character at position i, we need 0 operations
- If exactly two strings have the same character and one is different, we need 1 operation (change the different one)
- If all three strings have different characters, we need 2 operations (change two of them to match the third, or change two to match each other)

Wait, let me reconsider this. Actually, the minimum operations for each position would be:
- If all three are the same: 0 operations
- If exactly two are the same: 1 operation (change the different one)
- If all three are different: 2 operations (we can change two of them to match the third)

Actually, let me think about this more carefully. For each position, we want to make all three characters equal. The minimum number of changes needed is equal to the number of strings that have a different character at that position compared to the majority or to what we need.

More precisely: for each position i, we count how many of the three strings have different characters. If there are k strings with different characters at position i, we need k operations to make them all the same.

But actually, we can think of it as: if at position i, the characters are x, y, z, then we want to make them all equal to some character. The minimum number of changes needed is the number of strings that don't have the most frequent character at that position.

For example:
- If characters are a, a, b: two a's, one b → we only need 1 operation (change b to a)
- If characters are a, b, c: all different → we need 2 operations (change two of them to match the third)

So the rule is: at each position, we need (number of strings that have different characters from the majority character) operations.

But actually, it's simpler: at each position, we need the number of strings whose character doesn't match the most frequent character at that position.

Alternatively, we can say: at position i, if we have characters c1, c2, c3, then we want to make them all equal. The minimum number of changes is the number of characters that are not the most frequent one.

For each position, we can:
1. Count the frequency of each character
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. The number of operations needed at this position is (3 - maximum frequency)

Since the total number of strings is 3, if the maximum frequency is f, then (3 - f) strings need to be changed.

## Example Verification

Example 1: N=4, A="west", B="east", C="wait"
- Position 0: w, e, w → frequencies: w:2, e:1 → max=2 → operations = 3-2 = 1
- Position 1: e, a, a → frequencies: a:2, e:1 → max=2 → operations = 3-2 = 1  
- Position 2: s, s, i → frequencies: s:2, i:1 → max=2 → operations = 3-2 = 1
- Position 3: t, t, t → frequencies: t:3 → max=3 → operations = 3-3 = 0
- Total = 1+1+1+0 = 3 ✓

Example 2: All same → each position has max frequency 3 → operations = 0 ✓

Example 3: N=7, A="zenkoku", B="touitsu", C="program"
Let me verify quickly:
- Position 0: z, t, p → all different → max frequency = 1 → operations = 3-1 = 2
- Position 1: e, o, r → all different → operations = 2
- Position 2: n, u, o → all different → operations = 2
- Position 3: k, i, g → all different → operations = 2
- Position 4: o, t, r → all different → operations = 2
- Position 5: k, u, a → all different → operations = 2
- Position 6: u, s, m → all different → operations = 2
- Total = 2+2+2+2+2+2+2 = 14, but expected is 13.

Wait, I got 14 but expected is 13. Let me double-check position 5: A[5]='k', B[5]='u', C[5]='a' → all different → yes, 2 operations.
Position 6: A[6]='u', B[6]='s', C[6]='m' → all different → 2 operations.

Hmm, that gives 14. Let me recheck the example.

Wait, actually let me look again at A="zenkoku" - position 5 is 'k'? Let me count:
- z e n k o k u → positions: 0:z, 1:e, 2:n, 3:k, 4:o, 5:k, 6:u
- B="touitsu" → t o u i t s u → positions: 0:t, 1:o, 2:u, 3:i, 4:t, 5:s, 6:u
- C="program" → p r o g r a m → positions: 0:p, 1:r, 2:o, 3:g, 4:r, 5:a, 6:m

So:
- pos 0: z,t,p → all different → 2 operations
- pos 1: e,o,r → all different → 2 operations  
- pos 2: n,u,o → all different → 2 operations
- pos 3: k,i,g → all different → 2 operations
- pos 4: o,t,r → all different → 2 operations
- pos 5: k,s,a → all different → 2 operations
- pos 6: u,u,m → u appears twice! → u:2, m:1 → max=2 → operations = 3-2 = 1

Ah! Position 6 has u,u,m → two u's → operations = 1, not 2.

So total = 2+2+2+2+2+2+1 = 13 ✓

Perfect! So the algorithm is correct.

## Algorithm

For each position i from 0 to N-1:
1. Get the characters at position i from A, B, and C
2. Count the frequency of each character
3. Find the maximum frequency
4. Add (3 - maximum frequency) to the total operations

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = input().strip()
B = input().strip()
C = input().strip()

total_operations = 0

for i in range(N):
    # Get characters at position i
    chars = [A[i], B[i], C[i]]
    
    # Count frequency of each character
    freq = {}
    for char in chars:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    # Find maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Operations needed at this position = 3 - max_freq
    total_operations += 3 - max_freq

print(total_operations)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have three strings A, B, and C of length N. We want to make all three strings equal by changing characters. In each operation, we can change one character in one of the strings to any lowercase letter.

The goal is to find the minimum number of operations needed.

## Key Insight

For each position i (from 0 to N-1), we need to determine how many characters at that position are different across the three strings.

At each position i:
- If all three strings have the same character, we need 0 operations (already equal)
- If exactly two strings have the same character and one is different, we need 1 operation (change the different one)
- If all three strings have different characters, we need 2 operations (we need to change two of them to match the third)

Wait, let me think more carefully. Actually, at each position, we want to make all three strings have the same character. So:

- If all three characters are the same: 0 operations
- If exactly two are the same: 1 operation (change the different one to match)
- If all three are different: we need to change 2 characters to match one of them (so 2 operations)

Actually, we don't need to change 2 characters - we can change any two characters to match one of them, or we could change one to match another. The minimum operations needed at position i is the number of positions where the characters differ.

At position i:
- If A[i] == B[i] == C[i]: 0 operations
- If exactly two are equal, say A[i] == B[i] but C[i] is different: 1 operation (change C[i])
- If all three are different: we need to change 2 of them, so 2 operations

Actually, the minimum operations needed at position i is simply the count of characters that are different from each other.

More precisely: at position i, we have three characters. The minimum operations needed is the number of characters that are not equal to the majority character (or if there's no majority, the number of different characters).

The minimum operations at position i is: 3 - (number of unique characters that appear the same)

Actually, simpler: at position i, we need to change some characters to make them all equal. The optimal is to keep one character and change the others to match it. So we need to change (3 - max_frequency) characters at position i.

For example:
- All same: max_freq = 3, operations = 0
- Two same, one different: max_freq = 2, operations = 1  
- All different: max_freq = 1, operations = 2

So at each position i, the number of operations needed is 3 - (maximum frequency of the same character among A[i], B[i], C[i]).

## Algorithm

1. Read N, A, B, C
2. For each position i from 0 to N-1:
   - Count the frequency of characters at position i in A, B, C
   - Find the maximum frequency
   - Add (3 - max_frequency) to our total operations
3. Return the total operations

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: N=4, A="west", B="east", C="wait"
- Position 0: w, e, w → frequencies: w:2, e:1 → max=2 → operations = 1
- Position 1: e, a, a → frequencies: a:2, e:1 → max=2 → operations = 1  
- Position 2: s, s, i → frequencies: s:2, i:1 → max=2 → operations = 1
- Position 3: t, t, t → frequencies: t:3 → max=3 → operations = 0
- Total = 1+1+1+0 = 3 ✓

Example 2: All same → each position has max_freq=3 → operations=0 ✓

Example 3: "zenkoku", "touitsu", "program"
I'll just trust the logic since it matches the pattern.

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = input().strip()
B = input().strip()
C = input().strip()

total_operations = 0

for i in range(N):
    # Get the three characters at position i
    char_a = A[i]
    char_b = B[i]
    char_c = C[i]
    
    # Count frequencies of these three characters
    freq = {}
    freq[char_a] = freq.get(char_a, 0) + 1
    freq[char_b] = freq.get(char_b, 0) + 1
    freq[char_c] = freq.get(char_c, 0) + 1
    
    # Find maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Operations needed at this position = 3 - max_freq
    total_operations += 3 - max_freq

print(total_operations)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have three strings A, B, and C of length N. We want to make all three strings identical by changing characters. In each operation, we can change one character in one of the strings to any other lowercase letter.

The key insight is that we need to find the minimum number of operations to make all three strings equal.

## Analysis

For each position i (from 0 to N-1), we have three characters: A[i], B[i], and C[i].

To make all three strings equal at position i, we need to determine which character should be at that position in the final string. The optimal choice is the most common character among the three at that position.

For example:
- If we have positions with characters: 'w', 'e', 'w' - then 'w' appears twice, so we should make the final string have 'w' at that position
- If we have 'w', 'e', 'a' - all appear once, so we need to change 2 characters (since we need only one of them)

For each position i:
- Count frequency of each character among A[i], B[i], C[i]
- The number of operations needed at position i is 3 minus the frequency of the most common character
- Why? Because we only need to change the ones that don't match the most frequent character

## Step-by-step solution

1. Read N, A, B, C
2. For each position i from 0 to N-1:
   - Collect the three characters at position i
   - Find the most frequent character among them
   - Calculate operations needed for this position = 3 - frequency of most frequent character
3. Sum up all operations across all positions

## Example walkthrough

Example 1: N=4, A="west", B="east", C="wait"
- Position 0: 'w', 'e', 'w' → most frequent: 'w' (frequency 2) → operations = 3-2 = 1
- Position 1: 'e', 'a', 'a' → most frequent: 'a' (frequency 2) → operations = 3-2 = 1  
- Position 2: 's', 's', 'i' → most frequent: 's' (frequency 2) → operations = 3-2 = 1
- Position 3: 't', 't', 't' → most frequent: 't' (frequency 3) → operations = 3-3 = 0
- Total: 1+1+1+0 = 3 ✓

Example 2: All strings are identical → every position has same characters → operations = 0 ✓

Example 3: N=7, A="zenkoku", B="touitsu", C="program"
- We need to calculate each position and sum up.

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = input().strip()
B = input().strip()
C = input().strip()

total_operations = 0

for i in range(N):
    chars = [A[i], B[i], C[i]]
    # Count frequency of each character
    freq = {}
    for char in chars:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Operations needed at this position = 3 - max frequency
    operations_at_pos = 3 - max_freq
    total_operations += operations_at_pos

print(total_operations)
```

The algorithm works because at each position, we want to minimize the number of changes. The optimal strategy is to make all three strings have the same character at that position, and we should choose the character that appears most frequently among the three current characters. Any character that doesn't match this most frequent one needs to be changed.

- If all three strings have the same character (A[i] == B[i] == C[i]), then 0 operations are needed.
- If exactly two strings have the same character and one is different, then 1 operation is needed (we change the different one to match).
- If all three strings have different characters, then 2 operations are needed (we need to change two of them to match the third).

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we can choose any string and change any character, the optimal strategy is:
- For each position, we want all three strings to have the same character.
- We only need to change characters to match the majority character at that position.

Actually, the minimum operations at each position is the number of strings that have a different character than the majority.

More precisely:
- If all three are the same: 0 operations
- If exactly two are the same: 1 operation (change the different one)
- If all three are different: 2 operations (we need to change two of them to match one of the others)

But we don't have to change exactly two - we could change two of them to match the third, or we could change one of them to match the other two. Either way, the minimum operations needed is the count of characters that differ from the majority.

Actually, for any position, the minimum operations needed is the number of strings where the character differs from the other two. Since we can choose which character to make them all match, we want to minimize the changes.

The optimal choice is to make them all match the most frequent character at that position.

So at each position i:
- Count how many of A[i], B[i], C[i] are equal.
- If all 3 are equal: 0 operations

- If all 3 are different: 2 operations (we need to change 2 of them to match the third, or change 1 to match one of the others - but actually we need 2 changes since we can't make 3 different characters into 1 with fewer than 2 changes)

Wait, let me verify with the example:
- Position 0: w, e, w → w and w, e → two w's, one e → need 1 change (change e to w)

Actually, when there are 2 distinct characters, there is one character that appears twice and one that appears once. So we have 2 occurrences of the same character and one different. We only need to change the one different character to match the others. So 1 change.

So the formula per position is: 3 - (number of distinct characters) ???
Wait: 3-3=0, 3-2=1, 3-1=2. But 3-1=2, yes.

Actually, no: if we have 3 distinct characters, we need 2 changes. If we have 2 distinct characters, we need 1 change. If we have 1 distinct character, we need 0 changes.

So the number of changes needed at each position is: 3 - (the count of the most frequent character)?

No, it's simpler: it's the number of strings that have a character different from the others.

Actually, it's: total strings - the size of the largest group.

But if we have: A[i]=x, B[i]=x, C[i]=y → largest group is 2, so operations = 3-2 = 1 ✓
If A[i]=x, B[i]=y, C[i]=z → largest group is 1, so operations = 3-1 = 2 ✓
If A[i]=x, B[i]=x, C[i]=x → largest group is 3, operations = 3-3 = 0 ✓

Perfect!

So algorithm:
- For each position i from 0 to N-1:
  - Find the maximum frequency of any character among A[i], B[i], C[i]
  - Add (3 - max_frequency) to the total operations

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: N=4, A="west", B="east", C="wait"
- Position 0: w,e,w → frequencies: w:2, e:1 → max=2 → operations += 3-2 = 1
- Position 1: e,a,a → e:1, a:2 → max=2 → operations += 1  
- Position 2: s,s,i → s:2, i:1 → max=2 → operations += 1
- Position 3: t,t,t → t:3 → max=3 → operations += 0
- Total: 1+1+1+0 = 3 ✓

Example 2: all same → each position: max frequency = 3 → operations += 0 → total 0 ✓

Example 3: "zenkoku", "touitsu", "program"
I won't calculate all, but let's trust the logic.

So the solution is:

```python
n = int(input())
a = input().strip()
b = input().strip()
c = input().strip()

operations = 0
for i in range(n):
    # Get the three characters at position i
    chars = [a[i], b[i], c[i]]
    # Count frequency of each character
    freq = {}
    for char in chars:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    # Find maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Operations needed at this position = 3 - max_frequency
    operations += 3 - max_freq

print(operations)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have three strings A, B, and C of length N. We want to make all three strings identical by changing characters. In each operation, we can choose one string and change one character to any lowercase letter.

The key insight is that we want to minimize the total number of operations. For each position i (from 0 to N-1), we need to determine how many changes are needed to make all three strings have the same character at that position.

## Step-by-Step Analysis

For each position i, we have three characters: A[i], B[i], and C[i].

There are three possible outcomes for position i:
1. All three characters are already the same → 0 operations needed
2. Two characters are the same, one is different → 1 operation needed (change the different one)
3. All three characters are different → 2 operations needed (change two of them to match the third, or change two to match one)

Actually, let me think more carefully about case 3. If all three are different, we can:
- Change two characters to match the third → 2 operations
- Change one character to match another → 1 operation (but then the other two need to be changed)

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we want to minimize operations, at each position we want to make all three characters equal.

For position i:
- If A[i] = B[i] = C[i]: 0 operations
- If exactly two are equal: 1 operation (change the different one to match the other two)
- If all three are different: 2 operations (we need to change at least 2 of them to match one of the others)

Actually, no - we can change just one character at a time. If all three are different, we need to change at least two characters to make them all equal (since we start with three different values). For example, if we have 'a', 'b', 'c', we can either:
- Change 'a' and 'b' to 'c': 2 operations
- Change 'a' and 'c' to 'b': 2 operations  
- Change 'b' and 'c' to 'a': 2 operations

So in all cases where not all three are equal, we need exactly 1 or 2 operations?

Wait, let me check: if two are equal and one is different, we need 1 operation (change the different one to match the other two).

If all three are different, we need 2 operations (change two of them to match the third).

So the pattern is:
- If all three characters are the same: 0 operations
- If exactly two are the same: 1 operation  
- If all three are different: 2 operations

This means we can compute the total operations by iterating through each position and summing up:
- 0 if all three characters are identical
- 1 if exactly two characters are identical
- 2 if all three characters are different

But wait, is this optimal? Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: N=4, A="west", B="east", C="wait"
Position 0: 'w','e','w' → two 'w's, one 'e' → 1 operation
Position 1: 'e','a','a' → two 'a's, one 'e' → 1 operation  
Position 2: 's','s','i' → two 's's, one 'i' → 1 operation
Position 3: 't','t','t' → all same → 0 operations
Total: 1+1+1+0 = 3 ✓

Example 2: All strings are identical → 0 operations ✓

Example 3: "zenkoku", "touitsu", "program"
Let me check a few positions:
Position 0: 'z','t','p' → all different → 2 operations
Position 1: 'e','o','r' → all different → 2 operations
Position 2: 'n','u','o' → all different → 2 operations
Position 3: 'k','i','g' → all different → 2 operations
Position 4: 'o','t','r' → all different → 2 operations
Position 5: 'k','s','a' → all different → 2 operations
Position 6: 'u','u','m' → two 'u's → 1 operation

Total: 2+2+2+2+2+2+1 = 13 ✓

Perfect! This matches the expected output.

## Algorithm

1. Read N, A, B, C
2. For each position i from 0 to N-1:
   - Compare A[i], B[i], C[i]
   - If all three are equal: add 0 to total
   - If exactly two are equal: add 1 to total
   - If all three are different: add 2 to total
3. Return total

We can implement this using a simple loop and character comparison.

```python
N = int(input())
A = input().strip()
B = input().strip()
C = input().strip()

total_operations = 0

for i in range(N):
    # Get the three characters at position i
    char_a, char_b, char_c = A[i], B[i], C[i]
    
    # Count how many are the same
    if char_a == char_b == char_c:
        # All three are the same
        operations = 0
    elif char_a == char_b:
        # First two are same, third is different
        operations = 1
    elif char_a == char_c:
        # First and third are same, second is different
        operations = 1
    elif char_b == char_c:
        # Second and third are same, first is different
        operations = 1
    else:
        # All three are different
        operations = 2
    
    total_operations += operations

print(total_operations)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have three strings A, B, and C of length N. We want to make all three strings identical by changing characters. In each operation, we can choose one string and change one character to any other lowercase letter.

The key insight is that we want to minimize the total number of operations. For each position i (from 0 to N-1), we need to figure out how many changes are needed to make all three strings have the same character at that position.

## Analysis

For each position i, we have three characters: A[i], B[i], and C[i].

To make all three strings equal at position i, we need to change characters until they all match. The minimum operations needed at position i depends on how many of the three characters are already the same.

Let's consider the possible cases for position i:

1. If all three characters are the same (A[i] == B[i] == C[i]), then we need 0 operations.

2. If exactly two characters are the same, say A[i] == B[i] but C[i] is different, then we need 1 operation (change C[i] to match A[i] or B[i]).

3. If all three characters are different, then we need 2 operations (we need to change two of them to match the third).

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, we can only change one character at a time, and we can choose which string to change. For each position, we want to determine the minimum number of changes needed to make all three strings have the same character at that position.

The optimal strategy for each position is:
- If all three characters are the same: 0 operations
- If exactly two characters are the same: 1 operation (change the different one to match the two that are the same)
- If all three characters are different: 2 operations (we need to change two of them to match the third)

Let me verify with the examples:

Example 1: N=4, A="west", B="east", C="wait"
- Position 0: 'w','e','w' → two 'w's, one 'e' → 1 operation
- Position 1: 'e','a','a' → two 'a's, one 'e' → 1 operation  
- Position 2: 's','s','i' → two 's's, one 'i' → 1 operation
- Position 3: 't','t','t' → all same → 0 operations
- Total: 1+1+1+0 = 3 ✓

Example 2: All strings are identical → 0 operations ✓

Example 3: Let me check "zenkoku", "touitsu", "program"
- Position 0: 'z','t','p' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 1: 'e','o','r' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 2: 'n','u','o' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 3: 'k','i','g' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 4: 'o','t','r' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 5: 'k','s','a' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 6: 'u','u','m' → two 'u's → 1 operation
- Total: 2+2+2+2+2+2+1 = 13 ✓

Perfect! The pattern works.

## Solution Approach

For each position i from 0 to N-1:
- Count how many of A[i], B[i], C[i] are the same
- If exactly 1 is the same → 2 operations (need to change 2 characters)
- If exactly 2 are the same → 1 operation (change 1 character)
- If all 3 are the same → 0 operations

Actually, I can simplify this: the number of operations needed at position i is equal to the number of characters that are different from the majority character, or simply: 3 minus the count of matching characters.

Wait, that's not quite right. The minimum operations is the number of characters that need to be changed. If we have 3 characters, and k of them are the same, then we need to change (3-k) characters to match the common one.

However, if there are 2 that are the same, we need 1 change. If there are 3 that are the same, we need 0 changes. If all are different, we need 2 changes (we can choose any one to be the target, then change the other two).

So the operation count at position i is:
- If all 3 equal: 0
- If exactly 2 equal: 1  
- If all different: 2

This is equivalent to: if the three characters have duplicates, then we need 3 - (number of distinct characters), but actually it's simpler:

The minimum operations needed = 3 - (maximum frequency of any single character at that position)

Actually, let's think: if we have 3 characters, and we want to make them all equal, we need to change all characters that don't match the majority. So if there's a character that appears 2 times, we change 1 character. If all are different, each appears once, so we change 2 characters.

So the formula is: operations at position i = 3 - (frequency of the most frequent character at position i)

Let me verify:
- All same: frequency = 3, operations = 3-3 = 0 ✓
- Two same: frequency = 2, operations = 3-2 = 1 ✓  
- All different: frequency = 1, operations = 3-1 = 2 ✓

Perfect! This works.

## Code Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = input().strip()
B = input().strip()
C = input().strip()

total_operations = 0

for i in range(N):
    # Get the three characters at position i
    chars = [A[i], B[i], C[i]]
    
    # Count frequency of each character
    freq = {}
    for char in chars:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    # The maximum frequency at this position
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Operations needed = total characters - max frequency
    operations_needed = 3 - max_freq
    
    total_operations += operations_needed

print(total_operations)
```

## Problem Understanding

We have three strings A, B, and C of length N. We want to make all three strings identical by changing characters. In each operation, we can choose one string and change one character to any lowercase letter.

The goal is to find the minimum number of operations needed.

## Key Insight

For each position i (from 0 to N-1), we have three characters: A[i], B[i], and C[i].

For each position, we need to determine the minimum cost to make all three characters at that position equal.

At position i, we have three choices:
1. Change A[i] to match B[i] and C[i] - cost: 2 (if A[i] ≠ B[i] or A[i] ≠ C[i])
2. Change B[i] to match A[i] and C[i] - cost: 2 (if B[i] ≠ A[i] or B[i] ≠ C[i])
3. Change C[i] to match A[i] and B[i] - cost: 2 (if C[i] ≠ A[i] or C[i] ≠ B[i])

- Make all three equal to A[i]: cost = number of positions where B[i] ≠ A[i] + number of positions where C[i] ≠ A[i]
- Make all three equal to B[i]: cost = number of positions where A[i] ≠ B[i] + number of positions where C[i] ≠ B[i]  
- Make all three equal to C[i]: cost = number of positions where A[i] ≠ C[i] + number of positions where B[i] ≠ C[i]

Since we're only changing one character at a time, and we want to minimize operations, for each position we should choose the character that appears most frequently at that position.

Actually, let me verify: if we have three different characters at a position, we need to change two of them to match one of the others. That takes 2 operations. If two are the same, we need to change one to match the other, taking 1 operation. If all three are the same, 0 operations.

Actually, the frequency at each position is either 1 or more. Since we have exactly three characters, the max frequency is at least 1.

I think I have to go with my algorithm. It seems logically sound.

So the solution is:
For each position i from 0 to N-1:
- Let chars = [A[i], B[i], C[i]]
- Find the maximum frequency of any character in chars
- Add (3 - max_frequency) to total operations

This gives the minimum operations needed.

```python
n = int(input())
a = input().strip()
b = input().strip()
c = input().strip()

total_operations = 0
for i in range(n):
    chars = [a[i], b[i], c[i]]
    # Count frequency of each character
    freq = {}
    for char in chars:
        freq[char] = freq.get(char, 0) + 1
    
    # Find maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    # Operations needed = 3 - max_frequency
    total_operations += 3 - max_freq

print(total_operations)
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given three strings A, B and C. Each of these is a string of length N consisting of lowercase English letters.

Our objective is to make all these three strings equal. For that, you can repeatedly perform the following operation:

* Operation: Choose one of the strings A, B and C, and specify an integer i between 1 and N (inclusive). Change the i-th character from the beginning of the chosen string to some other lowercase English letter.



What is the minimum number of operations required to achieve the objective?

Constraints

* 1 \leq N \leq 100
* Each of the strings A, B and C is a string of length N.
* Each character in each of the strings A, B and C is a lowercase English letter.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have three strings A, B, and C of length N. We want to make all three strings identical by changing characters. In each operation, we can choose one string and change one character to any lowercase letter.

The goal is to find the minimum number of operations needed.

## Key Insight

For each position i (from 0 to N-1), we need to determine how many characters differ among the three strings at that position.

- If all three strings have the same character at position i, then no operation is needed for that position.
- If exactly two strings have the same character at position i, then we need exactly 1 operation to change the third string to match the common character.
- If all three strings have different characters at position i, then we need 2 operations (we can change two of them to match the third, or change two to match each other).

Actually, let me think more carefully:

For each position i:
- If A[i] == B[i] == C[i]: 0 operations needed
- If exactly two are equal, say A[i] == B[i] ≠ C[i]: we need 1 operation (change C[i] to match A[i] or B[i])
- If all three are different: we need 2 operations (we can change any two to match the third, or change two to match each other)

Wait, let me reconsider: if all three are different, do we need 2 operations? Actually, yes - we need to change at least two characters to make them equal.

For example: if we have 'a', 'b', 'c' at a position:
- We need to change two of them to match the third (e.g., change 'b' and 'c' to 'a', or change 'a' and 'c' to 'b', etc.)
- This takes exactly 2 operations

So the pattern is:
- All same: 0 operations
- Exactly two same: 1 operation  
- All different: 2 operations

## Algorithm

1. Read N, A, B, C
2. For each position i from 0 to N-1:
   - Check the characters A[i], B[i], C[i]
   - Count how many are different
   - If all three are same: add 0 to total operations
   - If exactly two are same: add 1 to total operations
   - If all three are different: add 2 to total operations
3. Return the total operations

Alternatively, we can think of it as: for each position, we need to change all characters that don't match the majority (if any) or all that don't match one of the duplicates.

Actually, the simpler way is: at each position, we need to change as many characters as needed to make all three equal. 

If all three are the same: 0
If exactly two are the same: 1 (change the third to match)
If all three are different: 2 (change any two to match one of them)

This is correct.

## Verification with examples

Example 1: N=4, A="west", B="east", C="wait"
- Position 0: 'w','e','w' → two 'w', one 'e' → 1 operation
- Position 1: 'e','a','a' → two 'a', one 'e' → 1 operation  
- Position 2: 's','s','i' → two 's', one 'i' → 1 operation
- Position 3: 't','t','t' → all same → 0 operations
- Total: 1+1+1+0 = 3 ✓

Example 2: All same → 0 operations ✓

Example 3: "zenkoku", "touitsu", "program"
I'll verify one position: 
- Position 0: 'z','t','p' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 1: 'e','o','r' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 2: 'n','u','o' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 3: 'k','i','g' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 4: 'o','t','r' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 5: 'k','s','a' → all different → 2 operations
- Position 6: 'u','u','m' → two 'u' → 1 operation
- Total: 2*6 + 1 = 13 ✓

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
N = int(input())
A = input().strip()
B = input().strip()
C = input().strip()

operations = 0
for i in range(N):
    chars = [A[i], B[i], C[i]]
    # Count unique characters
    unique_chars = len(set(chars))
    
    if unique_chars == 1:
        # All same
        operations += 0
    elif unique_chars == 2:
        # Exactly two same, one different
        operations += 1
    else:  # unique_chars == 3
        # All different
        operations += 2

print(operations)
```
